So, the first thing that we could do there to test out to see what the problem was was maybe use that hook again in another video or go back and iterate on that concept and change the hook for something else and see if it affects the performance. because we're looking at these sections in the ads more like building blocks. It allows us to switch things out like Lego. So, let's say that we tried that the hook didn't work still and the performance is still slacking. Well, maybe this time we can take that script and we can put it in a different format. Perhaps we just do a voice over with never seeing the UGC creator. Again, we test it. We see what the results are because the core phases of this production engine are we want to import something. We want to produce something afterwards. We want to test it followed by feedback on our tests and then finally expansion. Where can we go with that original idea? This is why a creative system runs continuously instead of a traditional marketing campaignled level. Because if we look at like the print media days, madmen times, normally what they did is they launched a billboard or a TV commercial and then that was it. They worked in these like campaign sprints. In fact, if your hit rate is that high, you might be choking your own growth. Here is the biggest issue with hit rate that nobody talks about. It's a binary metric in a non-binary game. Hit rate doesn't account for volume, scale, cost, efficiency. It doesn't account for any of your time. It doesn't account for anything else. Hit rate treats every win as equal. So, let's talk about a better, safer way to test landing pages with ads. Don't AB test new landing pages outside of meta, right? If you want to test small changes, I don't care. But if we're talking about like big swings or big changes to copy, big changes to visuals, or totally net new, especially net new landing pages, right? Test them with dedicated ads. Take your top evergreen, your best ads, and when I say that, I just want to be clear. Your top spending ads from whatever recent period or otherwise relevant ads to what you're trying to make a landing page for, right? If if you're just if your top spending ads aren't about this new thing that you're putting in the landing page in for, then don't use those. Use the most relevant ads. But you want to ideally take your top evergreen ads with the same creative, same copy, same targeting. Duplicate them into identical new ad sets or campaigns, whatever it is. Pair each of those ads with the new landing page variant. So you're going to have a bundle of ads. All of them are going to have the new landing page or the variant, whatever it is you're you're testing. And then let Meta optimize that combination independently. Like let it just spend money on that. You want to minimize all your other variables. You don't want to change the targeting. You don't want to like change your bid structure. You don't want to change how you're doing. You want it to have the same chance that whatever however you've been advertising this your existing page, you want to give it the same chance to succeed. But you want to also hopefully you're targeting broad or even if you're not targeting broad, the system is going to go target it broad for you. You want the system to be able to find the users that are more relevant to it. So even if your ads are the same, right? Even if you have the same ads, it's very possible that a subset of users who view that ad and then go to your site are going to be a different subset of users that it's more relevant to. So, here are some important notes about this. This method can, you know, if you were to do this, right? You're just creating new ads for every new landing page. It can create an excess amount of ads. And I would not recommend this for advertisers with limited conversion data. If you have a really high AOV product and you're not spending a lot of money and you're not getting a lot of conversion data, creating more ads is creating less consolidation. you have more and more things that the system has to handle and optimize for and you're manually having to control and it's not going to be good. But the more data you have, if you're either spending a ton of money or if you have a a lower cost pro product and you're selling a lot of it or just somewhere in the middle and you're getting lots of data, then you can afford to be doing this and you should be doing it this way. So just be mindful of that. And also with any of this, you have to be mindful of the audience warmth and your targeting exclusions or the lack of targeting exclusions and how meta might favor warmer audiences and how ads and pages that favor warmer audiences, they might not be what scales your brand best over time. So, you can always keep that in your back pocket. So, main takeaways here, landing pages affect more than just conversion rate because they influence who Meta sends ads to next. Their relevance is dynamic and adaptive, and small landing page changes can shift performance dramatically. The best way to test landing pages is to pair each variant with its own ads rather than splitting traffic. Sometimes having both page A and page B is better than choosing one over the other. And split testing isn't always as important as scaling, right? We're not scientists. Even if you are a scientist, I don't care. You're not a scientist here. We are trying to make more money. And learning sometimes you can learn while letting the system handle it for you. Minimize other variables. This is one of the biggest keys and one of the biggest things I teach and practice is just trying to minimize variables at all times. When I'm making a change to something, don't change other things too. So you can get that learning right. So, I'm simultaneously saying don't be a scientist, but also maybe be a little scientific in in terms of just not conflating variables with each other. I think that's just common sense and I've just been bitten too many times by people making too many changes at a time where they'll change something on the landing page, change a bid, change a setup, too many things. Do what you can to streamline and minimize other variables so that you can better understand what's going on. And in this case, give each landing page a fair shot at finding its ideal audience. And also go retest some of your past tests and variants and landing pages. There's very possible that you have something that you've run and tested and didn't do well that you can actually go and retest in this way and you might find some massive massive wins. Especially like really think about you know what that big swing we took and it didn't work out maybe this is why. So now go out there and make some more money. I thank you very much. Go land those Go land those pages. I've been Barry hot. Thank you for for tuning in here. I hope this was valuable. - You're wasting too much money in the learning phase: Kind: captions Language: en You do not need 50 conversions to know whether or not a creative test or concept is successful or a failure. I'm Barry Hot. I've been advertising on Facebook for 13 years. I've spent hundreds of millions of dollars and I've brought in billions of dollars in revenue for my clients. And I want to talk about the learning phase and why it's not necessary to complete a learning phase for every new creative test. We have the ability to look at secondary and tertiary metrics instead of just the one metric that we have in Facebook as like the conversion typically purchase. When we're optimizing to purchases, we also know that our ads are generating people into the they're putting people into the funnel that are adding to cart, initiating checkout, and converting. And if we know how much it costs for someone to add to cart and we know how much it costs for someone to initiate checkout, we know those ratios and we know uh the rates and we know the costs. We as advertisers don't need to spend the full amount of money that it takes to get a learning phase completed to know if an a new concept works. Meaning typically for us, we'll put uh several new ads that is or testing a concept into an adset and we will test those against each other and we don't need to spend 50 times the cost per purchase to know if they're going to work. If we have a goal cost per purchase of $50, that means that we need to spend $2500 to complete the learning phase. But that's only if the the concept that we're testing has that good of a CPA. If the CPA is $100, that means it's going to cost us $5,000 to test. If the CPA is $200, that means we're going to have to spend $10,000 to get the 50 conversions. What we do when we're testing is by the time we've we've spent enough to have gotten 10 conversions through what our goal CPA is, and again, in this instance, we're saying 50 $50 CPA. So, if we've spent $500, we now know what the CPA for this asset is. This isn't perfect, and we know that Facebook hasn't completed probably testing everything it can yet, but we know it's spent $500. We know it's gotten a ton of impressions and we know it's also driven other actions like add to cart and initiate checkout. If my goal CPA is $50 and I've seen for that account that my typical cost per add toart is $5. If we've spent $500, I know if I'm above or below my CPA and I also know if I'm above or below my estimated expected cost per ad to cart. If I see that we have uh gotten a $200 CPA by the time we've spent $500 or let's say $250 CPA just for easier math. We've gotten two conversions after spending $500. I'm going to take a look at my add toarts and if I can see that my add to carts are also doing that poorly compared to my cost per purchase. Right? That same they're doing very badly. That means that that creative concept is either not getting people's attention, it's not getting people to click, or it's not getting people to convert or pay, you know, do enough to get them to convert. So, I don't need to continue spending beyond that $500. That $500 has given me enough data and info to know this concept is not worth continuing to spend on because if I do want to continue to spend on it, I have to spend 50 times $250 to be able to complete that that learning phase. if it's a $250 CPA. So, why would I spend that when I can end that there and move that budget to something else that's already performing better or move that budget to testing something else? So, we're always looking at secondary and tertiary metrics to be able to help us decide whether or not we should continue spending on something. And we mostly use it as a determinant of whether or not we should turn off a test or if we should push more budget into it to be able to test it more rapidly and get those results quicker. That's how I uh do it and that's that's what's worked for me and allows me to test and scale at scale both for for big clients and small clients so that we can move more rapidly through creative testing. Let me know if you have any thoughts or questions about that. Thanks.

- This "best performing campaign ever" was actually a failure: Kind: captions Language: en story time this is about the rhode island it's a moment in my career where i learned a lot about breakdowns and misunderstanding data i was working for a direct consumer brand i was asked to set up a campaign identical to one that ran the year before so i was like okay i mean i see that campaign did really well i get it we'll set it up even though i don't agree with why it would work but you know what maybe i'm wrong let's challenge that so i duplicate the whole setup we run it again same creative same everything and it tanks it does worse than everything else we're already running so we we cut it and i go and take a look at why did it do so well last year i look at place and breakdowns age gender and i decide let me look at region breakdowns let me see what states are popping off here that's where i hit the jackpot the state that was driving the most conversions was the home state of that company rhode island what we discovered was that when customers were calling to place an order or needing help placing order customer service in rhode island would place the order on the website and those people had been exposed to facebook ads and because of at the time the 28 day click attribution someone could have clicked an ad and then been responsible for dozens if not hundreds but dozens of conversions and thousands of dollars in revenue and because of how it was attributed back then it was all attributed to like one day or a couple days because it was attributed to the day of the impression not the day of the conversion was able to realize no that campaign that we we thought did well didn't do well um which is a common problem in advertising people look at data and they believe it they take it as truth you don't double click into it because especially because it's good data performance looks great why you know people are just like oh that's great big learning from there make sure you look at your replacement breakdowns if something is seemingly too good to be true or too bad to be true you might find something that's not working well additionally just try and be thoughtful about like how something just anything can can muck up your data like that that's that's more qualitative than quantitative and don't just take your numbers for what they are it's really easy to just trust the numbers because they're numbers and numbers don't lie people say stuff like that but in reality humans make mistakes and humans make mistakes that impact what numbers you look at so remember to double click in try and understand what your data means and hopefully you'll uh not make the same mistakes that i did
